corpus/knowledge/pz-reporting-transparency-trial.md:68: Lane reading: two different delays, and the trial targets the first. The user-side gap (L17) is deployment lag after a fix exists; the upstream gap (L17) is integration lag between an upstream fix and a downstream product. The F06 frontier row already teaches the downstream caution in its own words — "an upstream fix is not downstream evidence" (`corpus/README.md:207`) — and S017 supplies the mechanism behind that caution: the downstream party may not know a fix exists, which is why the post's intervention is a report-existence signal.
corpus/knowledge/pz-reporting-transparency-trial.md:82: The benefit claims are hopes and beliefs (L31, L35), and the measurable claim is about tracking (L32), not about outcomes: the post announces a data source before the trial produces data. The cost side is not denied — vendors whose bugs only they can fix get attention they did not ask for — and the post's answer is a judgement about a minority ("these vendors now represent the minority of vulnerabilities reported by Project Zero", L35), not an exemption for them. This is a self-reported, first-party expectation and must be labelled as such (Q4).

corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:11: Japan's vulnerability intake is a **state-coordinated** pipeline, not a marketplace: IPA receives the report as the designated intake body, and JPCERT/CC contacts the product developer and runs the publication adjustment. IPA dates the arrangement to a Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry notification: "2004年7月8日から経済産業省の告示に基づき" (S042.txt:130), and states its own role as "IPA は、届出受付機関として届出された脆弱性関連情報を下記のとおり取扱います。" (S042.txt:131). There is no bounty and no per-finding severity ratchet; the finder's operative artefact is a **date**.

corpus/knowledge/cisa-when-to-issue-vex.md:38: An initial `under_investigation`, including one issued while the supplier is "surprised by the disclosure" (L110-L111), is the expected first move; it is superseded by an update, not left standing. Refinement is continuous: "As the supplier refines its understanding of the vulnerability, the supplier should update or issue additional VEX information." (L100-L101). Later changes are not limited to status: "VEX can also convey changes to remediation actions (“action_statement”) and further details about “not_affected” status (“impact_statement”)." (L139-L141).
corpus/knowledge/csaf-vex-product-status-and-justifications.md:41: `fixed` and `known_not_affected` are therefore different claims about different versions: a fixed version contains the fix, while a not-affected version needs no remediation — "No remediation is required regarding this vulnerability." (L1890).
corpus/knowledge/csaf-vex-product-status-and-justifications.md:53: > "additional product specific information SHALL be provided in /vulnerabilities[]/remediations as an action statement." — L2153
corpus/knowledge/csaf-vex-product-status-and-justifications.md:55: Test 6.1.27.10 makes it checkable: "it MUST be tested that a corresponding action statement exist in /vulnerabilities[]/remediations" (L2933). And `known_affected` itself recommends action: "Actions are recommended to remediate or address this vulnerability." (L1888).

corpus/knowledge/cisa-bod-20-01-vdp-minimums.md:86: ## Claim 6 — the 45-90 day public-disclosure norm is described as community practice, not as a rule
corpus/knowledge/cisa-bod-20-01-vdp-minimums.md:88: > "Many in the security research community consider public disclosure of a vulnerability to be appropriate between 45 to 90 days" — L305
corpus/knowledge/cisa-bod-20-01-vdp-minimums.md:90: The same paragraph warns that a longer imposed delay "may not be unreasonable – and anything more than 90 days begins to veer away from what is reasonable" (L305), and it records that the directive lets an agency require a defined window but not an unreasonable one. corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:27: **The one-year withdrawal rule.** A finder who has asked IPA not to disclose (情報非開示依頼) can have that request lifted once a year has run: "なお、起算日4から 1 年以上経過した届出については、発見者は IPA に対し、" (S041.txt:305) "情報非開示依頼の取り下げを求めることができます。" (S041.txt:306). The operative clause repeats in the process list — "IPA は、起算日から 1 年以上経過した届出について、発見者から情報非開示依" (S041.txt:430, wrapping into "頼の取下げが求められた場合、これを取り下げます。", L431) — and adds that where the developer needs more time for a justified reason, IPA must explain the situation so the finder can judge the need to disclose. corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:43: 起算日 is this round's transferable artefact. The lane's timeline rule (F02, `corpus/knowledge/coordinated-disclosure-timelines.md`) asks a disclosure record to name its coordinator, the event that starts the clock, the stated interval, and any documented extension. 起算日 answers the trigger question differently from the F02 sources, whose recorded triggers are the report or the availability of a fix (F02, S004 and S005): here the trigger is an **attempt**, so the clock starts even if the developer never answers, and both sides can see the same date because IPA relays it to the finder. corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:86: 3. **Withdrawal rule.** A record claiming the non-disclosure request can be withdrawn before one year from 起算日 is falsified by "なお、起算日4から 1 年以上経過した届出については、発見者は IPA に対し、" (S041.txt:305).
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:91: - **Rejected: "Japan runs a fixed 45-day disclosure deadline."** The 45 days are 目安, subject to four stated considerations (S041.txt:648-653), changeable on the developer's request (S041.txt:660), and the publication can be called off on five grounds (S041.txt:662-671). A record that states 45 days as a deadline has dropped the capture's qualifier.
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:93: - **Rejected: "This is the Japanese standard for coordinated disclosure."** It is IPA's and JPCERT/CC's operation of the 早期警戒パートナーシップ under a METI notification (S042.txt:130), and the guideline's advice is addressed to the finders, developers and site operators inside that partnership — for example its legal-points annex opens its finder section with "１－１．脆弱性関連情報の発見に際しての法的な問題" (S041.txt:1338) and asks finders for evidence such as "「画面キャプチャ」、" (S041.txt:344). It binds those parties, not coordinators elsewhere.
corpus/knowledge/linux-kernel-security-bugs-reporting-process.md:9: This entry records one upstream project's reporting rule: the Linux kernel security team's page on how to report security bugs to the kernel. It governs reports sent to the kernel security list and to kernel maintainers. It is not a rule for distributions, for other projects, or for CVE handling, and it is not general guidance on coordinated disclosure.

corpus/knowledge/rfc-9116-security-txt.md:8: IETF Informational RFC, April 2022, E. Foudil and Y. Shafranovich; it defines a format and defers the rest of disclosure to other documents ("Other details of vulnerability disclosure are outside the scope of this document", S063.txt:L104 §1.1). Its own stated relation to a vendor's other resources is subordinate: "The \"security.txt\" file is intended to be complementary and not a substitute or replacement for other public resources" (S063.txt:L115 §1.1). Everything below is a rule of this file's format, never a claim about any named organization's practice.
corpus/knowledge/rfc-9116-security-txt.md:27: ## Claim 4 — `Policy` links the disclosure page, and the file itself lives under `/.well-known/`

corpus/knowledge/rfc-9116-security-txt.md:32: §3.1 repeats the division of labour: organizations SHOULD use this directive "to provide additional details regarding the scope and details of their vulnerability disclosure process", because the file carries a pointer while the page carries the prose — scope, authorization, timelines, rewards. The file's own location is §3's `/.well-known/security.txt` for a domain name or IP address, registered permanent in IANA's Well-Known URIs registry (§6.1). corpus/knowledge/ssvc-stakeholder-specific-vulnerability-categorization.md:50: The axes therefore stack rather than compete — this framing is lane analysis, not a quote from S054. F19's EPSS is a 0-1 probability that a published CVE will be exploited in 30 days (S053 L216) and is fed into Exploitation; F18's KEV is catalog membership carrying a per-record remediation clock (S052), usable as a decision point; F01's CVSS v4 scores severity (S001/S002). SSVC consumes such exploitation evidence and emits a stakeholder-specific action category (L262-L265) — it is neither a probability, nor a catalog listing, nor a severity metric, and this capture defines none of those three,
corpus/knowledge/ssvc-stakeholder-specific-vulnerability-categorization.md:54: **Falsifiable rule.** A statement about SSVC is falsified by any of: (a) presenting its output as a number, probability or severity band, when the outputs are four named categories (L262-L265) and `score` occurs 0 times; (b) equating Public PoC with active exploitation, when L289 defines None as no active exploitation *and* no public PoC while L295's Active requires use in the wild by real attackers; (c) assigning one priority to a vulnerability for every stakeholder, when L255 and L259 say the priority varies; (d) treating the ladder as mandatory, when L261 calls the categories examples; (e) 
corpus/knowledge/ssvc-stakeholder-specific-vulnerability-categorization.md:70: 1. **"SSVC replaces CVSS."** Rejected: SSVC rates "risk to the concerned stakeholder" and explicitly contrasts itself with systems that "rate on technical severity" (L247); CVSS appears only as separate model branches (L69-L70, L156-L157), and `score` occurs 0 times. Severity and prioritization answer different questions, and a CVSS metric is at most an input to a decision point, not an output of SSVC.
corpus/knowledge/ssvc-stakeholder-specific-vulnerability-categorization.md:71: 2. **"SSVC gives a risk score."** Rejected: the outputs are four named categories (L262-L265), and a decision point "is not representative of risk" (L277); the only `scoring` in the capture describes other systems (L473).
corpus/knowledge/ssvc-stakeholder-specific-vulnerability-categorization.md:74: 5. **"SSVC membership is KEV status."** Rejected: KEV occurs once, as the decision-point name "In KEV" (L153); KEV catalog status (F18/S052) is an input, and the output is a priority category, not a listing.
corpus/knowledge/ssvc-stakeholder-specific-vulnerability-categorization.md:75: 6. **"SSVC predicts exploitation."** Rejected: EPSS is the probability source and the site routes it as "input to Exploitation" (L93); SSVC's Exploitation takes evidence states (L289-L295), not a forecast.

corpus/knowledge/iso-29147-divergence-and-clause-crosswalk.md:13: JPCERT/CC's 2026-10 P-guideline (情報セキュリティ早期警戒パートナーシップガイドライン) states its own account of where its early-warning framework differs from ISO/IEC 29147:2014. The divergence is one sentence, and it is a difference in **obligation**, not in practice: the guideline does not require a vendor to adopt a vulnerability disclosure policy, while ISO/IEC 29147:2014 does require the policy and the publication of part of it (`S041.txt:1868-1870`). Everything else in 付録6 §4 is the operational list that follows from that difference: what a vendor that chooses ISO conformance must add on top of guideline-aligned
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:11: Japan's vulnerability intake is a **state-coordinated** pipeline, not a marketplace: IPA receives the report as the designated intake body, and JPCERT/CC contacts the product developer and runs the publication adjustment. IPA dates the arrangement to a Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry notification: "2004年7月8日から経済産業省の告示に基づき" (S042.txt:130), and states its own role as "IPA は、届出受付機関として届出された脆弱性関連情報を下記のとおり取扱います。" (S042.txt:131). There is no bounty and no per-finding severity ratchet; the finder's operative artefact is a **date**.
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:15: IPA's page numbers the product path 1.–10. (S042.txt:133-159). JPCERT/CC's guideline carries the same flow as 図３, "図３ ソフトウエア製品における脆弱性情報取扱いの全体フロー" (S041.txt:1281), announced by "ソフトウエア製品、ウェブアプリケーションそれぞれの脆弱性情報取扱いに関" (S041.txt:1157, wrapping into L1158).
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:17: 1. 届出の受付; 2. 受理／不受理の決定; 3. IPA → JPCERT/CC への連絡; 4. 製品開発者へ連絡 — an unreachable developer is named publicly rather than dropped: "製品開発者に連絡が取れない場合は、連絡不能開発者一覧に公表し、製品開発者からの連絡を呼びかけます。" (S042.txt:142), and a small-impact report can be closed at contact: "製品開発者に連絡することで取扱いを終了することがあります。" (S042.txt:143); 5. 起算日の連絡; 6. 製品開発者による対応 (a contact window, the verification result and a publication schedule, the mitigation, and status reports); 7. 対応状況の受付 — JPCERT/CC shares status with IPA and "発見者は、IPAへ進捗状況の問い合わせを行なう事ができます。" (S042.txt:153); 8. 公表日の調整; 9. 対応状況（対策方法）の公表 on the JVN portal; 10. "10. 統計情報の公表" (S042.tx
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:21: The finder is told a date, and JPCERT/CC defines what that date is: "本ガイドラインのⅣ．４．2)において規定された連絡を最初に試みた日を起算日とします。" (S041.txt:340). The anchor is the **first attempt** at the contact required by Ⅳ．4．2) — not the day IPA received the report, and not the day the developer answered.
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:23: IPA's step 5 relays it in both directions: "JPCERT/CC は製品開発者への連絡を開始した旨（起算日）" (S042.txt:145) goes to IPA, and "IPAは起算日、および、詳細情報連絡日を発見者へ連絡します。" (S042.txt:145) returns it to the finder.
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:25: JPCERT/CC sets a target publication date off that anchor: "みた日（起算日、２．注釈 4 を参照）から 45 日後を目安とします。ただし、" (S041.txt:648). The ただし clause is load-bearing — the figure is 目安 (a guide), not a deadline — and four considerations follow: "公表日の決定に際しては、以下の点も考慮します。" (S041.txt:649), from "① 対策方法の作成に要する期間" (S041.txt:650) to "④ 当該脆弱性の悪用に関するリスク" (S041.txt:653). A date already set can still move: JPCERT/CC may change it on the developer's request, "公表日を変更することがあります。" (S041.txt:660), and the clause then lists five grounds on which publication may be called off entirely (S041.txt:662-671).

corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:39: A product developer reporting its own product gets an eight-step path (S042.txt:184-200): IPA forwards to JPCERT/CC, JPCERT/CC contacts the developer named in the report, the developer sets a window and writes the mitigation, the publication date is adjusted, IPA and JPCERT/CC publish on JVN, and the last step is "8.統計情報の公表" (S042.txt:200). No 起算日 step appears here either.
corpus/knowledge/japanese-domestic-disclosure-workflow.md:43: 起算日 is this round's transferable artefact. The lane's timeline rule (F02, `corpus/knowledge/coordinated-disclosure-timelines.md`) asks a disclosure record to name its coordinator, the event that starts the clock, the stated interval, and any documented extension. 起算日 answers the trigger question differently from the F02 sources, whose recorded triggers are the report or the availability of a fix (F02, S004 and S005): here the trigger is an **attempt**, so the clock starts even if the developer never answers, and both sides can see the same date because IPA relays it to the finder. corpus/knowledge/japanese-severity-vocabulary-and-impact-model.md:12: JVN's 深刻度 labels are CVSS base-score bands; the national database does not define a severity scale of its own, and the JPCERT/CC guideline then derives handling from that score combined with impact scope. corpus/knowledge/japanese-severity-vocabulary-and-impact-model.md:18: **The handling model (S041).** The JPCERT/CC guideline's 付録4 frames impact-based handling: “脆弱性の深刻度（CVSSv3 基本値スコア）と影響範囲により決定します。” (S041.txt:1459), with “脆弱性の深刻度 ：脆弱性の深刻度として、CVSSv3 基本値スコアを適用する。” (S041.txt:1491). The scope factor is judged from ①利用者数, ②社会的影響力, ③攻撃の容易性, and “①～③がすべて不明” defaults to 広 (S041.txt:1492-1501). The result is the three-by-three response matrix at S041.txt:1465-1490 (簡易な対応 / 通常の対応 / 優先対応 by score band × scope). One closing rule for web applications: with a 4.0–6.9 base score, 中 scope, and a passive-only attack, handling may be terminated — “受動型の攻撃であれば、取扱いを終了することを可能とし” (S0
